## 4. How you and your partner push code without conflicts

The safest method is to use separate Git branches.

In your VS Code terminal, create your branch:

```bash
git switch -c greedy
```

Your partner creates their own:

```bash
git switch -c divide-conquer
```

Once you've written and tested your greedy code:

```bash
git add greedy/
git commit -m "Implement greedy algorithm and experiments"
git push -u origin greedy
```

Your partner will do:

```bash
git add divide_and_conquer/
git commit -m "Implement divide and conquer algorithm"
git push -u origin divide-conquer
```

Each of you then opens a **Pull Request** on GitHub from your branch into `main`. After reviewing and merging the pull requests, both of you can update your local copy:

```bash
git switch main
git pull origin main
```

This keeps the shared version organized and avoids accidentally overwriting unfinished work.

**Important:** Pull before starting new work on a shared branch, and don't use `git push --force` to solve synchronization problems.

## 5. Your Greedy Algorithm — What I recommend

For your 50-point portion, I recommend the following real-world problem:

**Maximizing the number of events scheduled in a single university classroom.**

This is an excellent greedy algorithm problem because it has a clear optimal solution, an understandable mathematical proof, and a running time you can verify experimentally.

<box gap={2}>
  <title size="lg">Example: Classroom Scheduling</title>
  A university has one classroom and receives requests for different events. Each event has a start and end time. Your goal is to schedule as many events as possible without overlaps.

  <box border radius="lg" padding={3} gap={2}>
    <svg viewBox="0 0 330 198" width="100%">
      {#each [0,1,2,3,4,5,6,7,8] as t}

      {/each}
      <rect x="12" y="182" width="9" height="9" rx="2" fill="#16a34a"/>
      <text x="26" y="190" fontSize="10" fill="currentColor">Selected</text>
      <rect x="103" y="182" width="9" height="9" rx="2" fill="#94a3b8" fillOpacity=".5"/>
      <text x="117" y="190" fontSize="10" fill="currentColor">Not selected</text>

    <caption>Illustrative scheduling requests, with start/end times shown in 24-hour format.</caption>
  </box>

  The optimal answer for this example is **3 events: A, C, and D**.
</box>

### Why is this a greedy problem?

A greedy algorithm makes the best local choice at every step.

For classroom scheduling, the greedy rule is:

**Always choose the compatible event that finishes earliest.**

Why? Because an event that finishes earlier leaves more time for other events to occur.

### Requirement 1 — Identify the real-world problem (10 pts)

Your problem is university classroom scheduling.

You should explain:

- Universities receive requests to reserve classrooms.
- Multiple requests can overlap.
- Only one event can use the classroom at a time.
- The objective is to maximize the number of approved events.

One important assumption: every event has equal value, and events cannot be interrupted or split. Otherwise, the optimal algorithm may be different.

### Requirement 2 — Mathematical abstraction (5 pts)

Now translate the real-world problem into a mathematical model.

Let:

\[
I=\{(s_1,f_1),(s_2,f_2),\ldots,(s_n,f_n)\}
\]

Where:

- \(I\) is the set of event requests.
- \(s_i\) is the starting time of event \(i\).
- \(f_i\) is its finishing time.
- \(n\) is the number of events.

Two events are compatible when they do not overlap. For example, event \(j\) can follow event \(i\) when \(s_j\geq f_i\).

Your objective is to find a maximum-cardinality subset \(S\subseteq I\) of mutually compatible events.

**Why this meets the requirement:** You've transformed classroom reservations into a set-selection problem, which is one of the abstractions your professor explicitly allows.

### Requirement 3 — The algorithm (10 pts)

Here's the pseudocode:

```text
GREEDY-CLASSROOM-SCHEDULING(events):

    Sort events by finish time (ascending)

    selected = empty list
    lastFinish = -infinity

    For each event in sorted events:

        If event.start >= lastFinish:
            Add event to selected
            lastFinish = event.finish

    Return selected
```

Let's test your understanding with an example:

| Event | Start | Finish |
|---|---:|---:|
| A | 9 | 11 |
| B | 10 | 13 |
| C | 11 | 12 |
| D | 12 | 15 |
| E | 14 | 17 |

The algorithm first selects A because it finishes at 11. It rejects B because B starts before A finishes. It selects C because C starts at 11, selects D because D starts at 12, and rejects E because E starts before D finishes.

Result: A, C, D.

### Requirement 3 — Running-time analysis (5 pts)

You need to analyze how the algorithm's work grows as the number of event requests increases.

| Operation | Complexity |
|---|---|
| Sort events by finishing time | \(O(n\log n)\) |
| Iterate through all events | \(O(n)\) |
| Check whether an event is compatible | \(O(1)\) per event |
| Store selected events | \(O(n)\) maximum space |

Therefore:

\[
T(n)=O(n\log n)+O(n)
\]

\[
\boxed{T(n)=O(n\log n)}
\]

The sorting step dominates the running time.

If the requests are already sorted by finishing time, the selection phase alone takes \(O(n)\).

### Requirement 3 — Proof of correctness (10 pts)

This is one of the most important parts of your assignment. It's not enough to show that your code produces a reasonable answer. You need to prove that it always produces an optimal schedule under the stated assumptions.

Use an **exchange argument**.

**Theorem:** Selecting the compatible event with the earliest finishing time at each step produces a maximum-cardinality schedule.

**Proof:**

Let \(g\) be the event with the earliest finishing time among all available events. Let \(O\) be an optimal schedule, with its events ordered by finishing time, and let \(o\) be its first event.

Because \(g\) has the earliest finishing time,

\[
f(g)\leq f(o).
\]

Replace \(o\) with \(g\) in the optimal schedule. Since \(g\) finishes no later than \(o\), every event that was compatible with \(o\) and came after it remains compatible with \(g\).

Thus, this replacement preserves the number of scheduled events. There is therefore an optimal schedule that starts with the greedy choice \(g\).

After choosing \(g\), the remaining problem consists of events starting at or after \(f(g)\). This is another instance of the same scheduling problem.

By repeatedly applying the exchange argument to the remaining events, we can construct an optimal schedule containing every event chosen by the greedy algorithm.

Therefore, the greedy algorithm returns a maximum-cardinality compatible schedule. \(\square\)

**What you should understand:** We aren't claiming that the shortest event is always best. We're proving that choosing the earliest-finishing event can never reduce the maximum number of events we can schedule.

### Requirement 4 — Explain the algorithm in classroom terminology (5 pts)

Here, you stop using the mathematical notation and explain what the university actually does.

The scheduling system receives reservation requests, arranges them by the time each event ends, and approves the event that frees the classroom earliest. It then continues approving requests that start after the previously approved event ends, rejecting conflicting requests. This maximizes the total number of events accommodated.

### Requirement 5 — Implement and experimentally verify the running time (5 pts)

You will write a C++ program and test it with increasing numbers of event requests.

For example:

| Input size \(n\) | Number of events generated |
|---|---:|
| 1 | 1,000 |
| 2 | 5,000 |
| 3 | 10,000 |
| 4 | 25,000 |
| 5 | 50,000 |
| 6 | 100,000 |
| 7 | 200,000 |

For each input size:

1. Generate random classroom requests.
2. Run your greedy algorithm.
3. Measure execution time using `std::chrono`.
4. Repeat the experiment several times.
5. Calculate the average or median running time.
6. Compare the measured growth against \(n\log_2 n\).

Your report should include a graph like this:

<box border radius="lg" padding={3} gap={2}>
  <text weight="medium" size="sm">Expected experimental result (illustrative only)</text>
  <Chart content={{"chartType":"line","meta":{"footer":"Illustrative normalized trend; not measurements from running code."},"xKey":"n","series":[{"dataKey":"predicted","label":"Expected O(n log n) growth"}],"yAxisMin":0,"data":[{"n":"1k","predicted":0.7},{"n":"5k","predicted":4.3},{"n":"10k","predicted":9.3},{"n":"25k","predicted":25.9},{"n":"50k","predicted":55.4},{"n":"100k","predicted":118.8},{"n":"200k","predicted":253.0}]}}/>
  <caption>You'll create a real plot with measured runtimes on the vertical axis and input sizes on the horizontal axis, plus a scaled theoretical curve for comparison.</caption>
</box>

You must actually run the program before claiming experimental results. Random input generation should happen outside the timed section. Since individual executions may finish quickly, repeating runs helps reduce noise.

## 6. Start implementing your greedy algorithm in VS Code

Create `greedy/greedy.cpp` and start with this working implementation:

<CodeBlock language="cpp" editable>
#include <algorithm>
#include <iostream>
#include <vector>
using namespace std;

struct Event {
    int id;
    int start;
    int finish;
};

vector<Event> scheduleEvents(vector<Event> events) {
    // Sort by earliest finishing time
    sort(events.begin(), events.end(),
         [](const Event& a, const Event& b) {
             return a.finish < b.finish;
         });

    vector<Event> selected;
    int lastFinish = -1;

    for (const auto& event : events) {
        if (event.start >= lastFinish) {
            selected.push_back(event);
            lastFinish = event.finish;
        }
    }

    return selected;
}

int main() {
    vector<Event> events = {
        {1, 9, 11},
        {2, 10, 13},
        {3, 11, 12},
        {4, 12, 15},
        {5, 14, 17}
    };

    vector<Event> result = scheduleEvents(events);

    cout << "Selected events:\n";

    for (const auto& event : result) {
        cout << "Event " << event.id
             << ": " << event.start
             << " - " << event.finish << '\n';
    }

    cout << "Total scheduled: " << result.size() << '\n';
    return 0;
}
</CodeBlock>

For this starter implementation, assume all event times are nonnegative.

If you have `g++` installed, compile and run it from the VS Code terminal:

```powershell
g++ -std=c++17 greedy/greedy.cpp -o greedy.exe
.\greedy.exe
```

Expected output:

```text
Selected events:
Event 1: 9 - 11
Event 3: 11 - 12
Event 4: 12 - 15
Total scheduled: 3
```

Once this works, the next programming task is to build `experiment.cpp` so you can measure runtime over thousands of generated events.

For example:
- **You:** VS Code + GitHub
- **Your partner:** VS Code, Visual Studio, CLion, or another editor + GitHub
- **Both:** Push code to the same GitHub repository using separate branches.

As long as your partner has **Git installed, a GitHub account, and access to your private repository**, you're good.


========== MESSAGE 019 — ASSISTANT — 2026-10-14T14:26:17.428764+00:00 ==========
**I actually like this idea more than classroom scheduling for your COT5405 greedy algorithm project.** You're taking a real-world problem you've noticed while driving and trying to improve it using algorithms.

What you're describing is a form of **traffic signal coordination**, sometimes called a *green wave*.

The idea is that traffic lights along a road should be coordinated so vehicles can move through multiple intersections without repeatedly stopping.

But there's an important distinction for your assignment: **we need to define a version of this traffic problem that a greedy algorithm can solve optimally**, because your professor specifically requires a proof of optimality.

## 1. Your proposed problem: Traffic Light Optimization

Imagine you're driving down a busy road with four intersections.

<box border radius="xl" padding={3} gap={2}>
  <text weight="medium">Example: Four consecutive intersections</text>
  <svg viewBox="0 0 330 154" width="100%">
    <rect x="13" y="57" width="304" height="42" rx="5" fill="#64748b" fillOpacity=".22"/>
    <path d="M15 78 H312" stroke="currentColor" strokeOpacity=".45" strokeDasharray="8 7" strokeWidth="1.5"/>
    {#each [46,126,206,286] as x,i}
      <rect x={x-12} y="36" width="24" height="83" rx="3" fill="#64748b" fillOpacity=".20"/>
      <rect x={x-9} y="28" width="18" height="27" rx="5" fill={i===3?"#16a34a":"#dc2626"} fillOpacity=".15" stroke={i===3?"#16a34a":"#dc2626"}/>
      <circle cx={x} cy="35" r="3.5" fill={i===3?"#64748b":"#dc2626"} fillOpacity={i===3?".35":"1"}/>
      <circle cx={x} cy="47" r="3.5" fill={i===3?"#16a34a":"#64748b"} fillOpacity={i===3?"1":".35"}/>
      <text x={x} y="17" fontSize="11" textAnchor="middle" fill="currentColor">L{i+1}</text>
    {/each}
    <path d="M96 137 H235" stroke="#16a34a" strokeWidth="2.5" strokeLinecap="round"/>
    <path d="M228 132 L237 137 L228 142" fill="none" stroke="#16a34a" strokeWidth="2.5" strokeLinecap="round"/>
    <text x="165" y="152" fontSize="10" fill="currentColor" textAnchor="middle">Direction of travel →</text>
  </svg>
  <caption>Illustrative signal states, not an actual coordinated timing plan.</caption>
</box>

Your observation is that if the farthest intersection is still red while upstream lights release more cars, the road can fill up.

**Your proposal:** Coordinate green lights to keep cars moving forward rather than releasing vehicles into congestion.

That is a legitimate transportation optimization problem.

## 2. How the timing should work

Your idea of activating the farthest light before vehicles reach it makes sense when a queue has built up there.

However, the lights don't necessarily need to turn green in reverse order. They should be timed based on vehicle travel time and whether traffic is already waiting downstream.

For example:

<box border radius="lg" padding={3} gap={2}>
  <title size="md">Example green-light schedule</title>
  <text color="secondary" size="xs">Hypothetical timing in seconds</text>
  <table>
    <table-row><table-cell>Light 1</table-cell><table-cell>0–30 s</table-cell></table-row>
    <table-row><table-cell>Light 2</table-cell><table-cell>8–38 s</table-cell></table-row>
    <table-row><table-cell>Light 3</table-cell><table-cell>16–46 s</table-cell></table-row>
    <table-row><table-cell>Light 4</table-cell><table-cell>24–54 s</table-cell></table-row>
  </table>
  <caption>Assumes approximately eight seconds of travel between intersections. A preexisting downstream queue could require an earlier green at that intersection.</caption>
</box>

This is consistent with the Federal Highway Administration's explanation of traffic-signal offsets. <Cite ref="turn690716search0"/>

## 3. How to turn this into your 50-point greedy assignment

Here's the tricky part: maximizing green-light time across a real road network isn't generally solved optimally by a simple greedy algorithm. You have cross traffic, pedestrians, competing directions, and intersection capacity to consider.

I would narrow your problem to:

**Greedy Selection of Non-Conflicting Green-Light Windows for Traffic Coordination**

You would assume a traffic management system has already generated a set of safe, candidate green-light windows for one constrained intersection or movement.

Each candidate has:

\[
W_i=(s_i,f_i)
\]

where \(s_i\) represents its beginning and \(f_i\) represents its ending.

Your goal would be to select the **maximum number of compatible green-light windows**, not the maximum total number of seconds of green.

The greedy rule is to select the available compatible window that finishes earliest.

This is mathematically equivalent to interval scheduling, so you can prove its optimality using an exchange argument and implement it in \(O(n\log n)\) time.

You could then experimentally verify its running time and separately use a small traffic simulation to illustrate how selected green windows affect vehicle movement.

One limitation you should explicitly acknowledge in the report: maximizing the number of compatible green windows does **not** guarantee minimum congestion or maximum vehicle throughput. Those would require a more sophisticated optimization model.

## 4. Which version do you actually want to build?

{@body const [choice,setChoice] = DIL.useState("coordination")}
<box border radius="xl" padding={3} gap={3}>
  <radio-group direction="col" value={choice} onChange={setChoice}>
    <radio value="coordination">
      **A. Coordinated traffic lights (your original idea)**

      Time consecutive green lights to help vehicles move along a road without repeatedly stopping. More realistic, but proving greedy optimality is harder.
    </radio>
    <radio value="windows">
      **B. Greedy green-light window scheduling**

      Choose the maximum number of compatible, pre-approved signal windows. Easier mathematical proof and a strong fit for your assignment.
    </radio>
  </radio-group>
  <button block onClick={()=>GenUI.issueNewTurn(choice==="coordination"?"I want option A: traffic light coordination. Help me formulate a meaningful simplified version with a provably optimal greedy algorithm if possible, and explain the tradeoffs.":"I want option B: greedy green-light window scheduling. Walk me through all five assignment requirements and the C++ implementation.")}>Continue with this problem <icon name="arrow-right" inline/></button>
</box>

My preference is **A for the real-world motivation**, but we need to be careful not to claim an optimal greedy solution without proving one. We could use B as the rigorously solvable subproblem within your broader traffic-coordination project.

========== MESSAGE 025 — ASSISTANT — 2026-10-14T14:32:10.182823+00:00 ==========
**Exactly! You got it.** You're understanding how the greedy algorithm makes its decisions.

There's just one small distinction: **we choose A because it has the earliest finish time (12 seconds), not necessarily because it starts at 0.**

Let's walk through your logic.

| Window | Start | Finish | Decision |
|---|---:|---:|---|
| A | 0 | 12 | <icon name="check-circle" color="success" inline/> Select first |
| B | 5 | 15 | <icon name="x-circle" color="danger" inline/> Overlaps A |
| C | 12 | 20 | <icon name="check-circle" color="success" inline/> Select second |
| D | 18 | 30 | <icon name="x-circle" color="danger" inline/> Overlaps C |
| E | 20 | 35 | <icon name="check-circle" color="success" inline/> Select third |

The resulting schedule is:

<box border radius="lg" padding={3} gap={2}>
  <svg viewBox="0 0 320 83" width="100%">
    {#each [0,5,10,15,20,25,30,35] as t}
      <line x1={14+t*8.2} y1="9" x2={14+t*8.2} y2="64" stroke="currentColor" strokeOpacity=".12"/>
      <text x={14+t*8.2} y="77" fontSize="9" fill="currentColor" textAnchor="middle">{t}s</text>
    {/each}
    {#each [{a:0,b:12,t:"A"},{a:12,b:20,t:"C"},{a:20,b:35,t:"E"}] as w}
      <rect x={14+w.a*8.2} y="22" width={(w.b-w.a)*8.2-2} height="33" rx="4" fill="#16a34a" fillOpacity=".85"/>
      <text x={14+(w.a+w.b)*4.1} y="42" fontSize="12" fill="white" textAnchor="middle" fontWeight="600">{w.t}</text>
    {/each}
  </svg>
  <text color="success" weight="medium" textAlign="center">A → C → E = 3 compatible windows</text>
</box>

### Now let's test something important

Suppose you have these three green-light windows:

| Window | Start | Finish |
|---|---:|---:|
| A | 0 | 15 |
| B | 2 | 8 |
| C | 8 | 12 |

**Which window should the greedy algorithm select first: A or B?**

Remember, the rule is to select the window that **finishes earliest**, even if another window starts earlier.

This distinction is important for understanding the algorithm and eventually writing your proof of correctness.


========== MESSAGE 026 — USER — 2026-10-14T14:32:32.445000+00:00 ==========
a


========== MESSAGE 027 — ASSISTANT — 2026-10-14T14:32:34.511601+00:00 ==========
Close! The correct answer is **B**, not A.

Remember, the greedy algorithm chooses the window with the **earliest finish time**, not the earliest start time.

| Window | Start | Finish | Decision |
|---|---:|---:|---|
| A | 0 | 15 | <icon name="x-circle" color="danger" inline/> Reject |
| B | 2 | 8 | <icon name="check-circle" color="success" inline/> Select first |
| C | 8 | 12 | <icon name="check-circle" color="success" inline/> Select second |

**Why B?**

- A starts first but doesn't finish until 15 seconds.
- B starts at 2 seconds and finishes at 8 seconds.
- C starts at 8 seconds, exactly when B finishes.

So selecting B allows us to select C afterward.

<box border radius="lg" padding={3} gap={2}>
  <svg viewBox="0 0 320 110" width="100%">
    {#each [0,2,4,6,8,10,12,14,16] as t}
      <line x1={35+t*16.5} y1="10" x2={35+t*16.5} y2="89" stroke="currentColor" strokeOpacity=".12"/>
      <text x={35+t*16.5} y="102" fontSize="9" fill="currentColor" textAnchor="middle">{t}</text>
    {/each}
    {#each [{name:"A",s:0,f:15,y:13,ok:false},{name:"B",s:2,f:8,y:40,ok:true},{name:"C",s:8,f:12,y:67,ok:true}] as w}
      <text x="10" y={w.y+16} fontSize="11" fill="currentColor">{w.name}</text>
      <rect x={35+w.s*16.5} y={w.y} width={(w.f-w.s)*16.5-2} height="21" rx="4" fill={w.ok?"#16a34a":"#94a3b8"} fillOpacity={w.ok?".85":".5"}/>
    {/each}
  </svg>
  <text weight="medium" color="success" textAlign="center">B → C = 2 windows</text>
</box>

If we chose A, we'd schedule only **1 window**. By choosing B first, we can schedule **2 windows**, which is optimal for this example.

**Quick question:** Why is choosing the event that starts earliest *not always* the best greedy strategy?

**Exactly! You're getting the idea.** Your reasoning is correct.

You recognized that selecting one long window can prevent us from selecting multiple shorter windows.

Here's the key distinction:

- **Our objective:** Maximize the *number* of compatible green-light windows.
- **Our greedy strategy:** Select the window that *finishes earliest*.
- **Why it works:** Finishing earlier leaves more opportunities to schedule additional windows.

One important point you made is that sometimes one long green-light cycle might accomplish the same traffic-management goal as two shorter cycles. That's true in real-world traffic control!

However, for **our assignment**, we're specifically optimizing the number of compatible windows, not necessarily traffic flow or the total time lights remain green.

## Let's test your understanding with a harder example

Suppose we have five possible green-light windows:

| Window | Start (s) | Finish (s) |
|---|---:|---:|
| A | 0 | 10 |
| B | 2 | 5 |
| C | 5 | 8 |
| D | 7 | 12 |
| E | 8 | 14 |

    <radio value="A-C-E">A → C → E</radio>
    <radio value="B-C-E">B → C → E</radio>

    <radio value="A-E">A → E</radio>

    <box gap={1}>
      <row align="center" gap={2}>
        <icon name={selection==="B-C-E"?"check-circle":"info"} color={selection==="B-C-E"?"success":"secondary"}/>

      </row>
      {#if selection==="B-C-E"}
        B finishes at 5, C starts at 5 and finishes at 8, and E starts at 8. All three are compatible.
      {:else}
        Look for the window that finishes earliest first, then select the next window whose start time is at least the previous finish time.
      {/if}
    </box>
  {/if}
  <button block onClick={()=>setChecked(true)}>Check answer</button>
</box>

## Step 3 — Write your README

You can paste this into `Greedy/README.md`. It's a starting description, not a claim that you've already completed the implementation or experiments.

<WritingBlock id="56321" variant="document"># Greedy Algorithm: Traffic Signal Window Scheduling

## 1. Problem Description
Traffic signal scheduling affects the movement of vehicles through intersections. Poorly coordinated signals can contribute to unnecessary stops and traffic congestion.

This project studies a simplified traffic signal scheduling problem using a greedy algorithm.

**Objective:** Select the maximum number of mutually compatible green-light windows from a set of candidate windows for a single traffic movement.

This model does not directly optimize vehicle throughput or guarantee reduced traffic congestion.

## 2. Mathematical Abstraction
Each candidate green-light window is represented by an interval:

\[
W_i = (s_i, f_i)
\]

Where:
- \(s_i\): Starting time of window \(i\).
- \(f_i\): Finishing time of window \(i\).
- \(n\): Total number of candidate windows.

Two consecutive selected windows are compatible when the next window starts at or after the previous window finishes.

The objective is to find the largest possible subset of mutually compatible windows.

## 3. Greedy Strategy
The algorithm selects candidate green-light windows in ascending order of finishing time.

For each window:
1. Check whether its starting time is greater than or equal to the ending time of the last selected window.
2. If compatible, select the window.
3. Otherwise, skip the window.

## 4. Expected Time Complexity
Sorting the windows requires O(n log n) time.

Processing all windows after sorting requires O(n) time.

Total expected time complexity: **O(n log n)**.

## 5. Implementation
- Language: C++
- Main algorithm: `greedy.cpp`
- Runtime experiments: `experiment.cpp`
- Experimental measurements: `results.csv`
- Experimental visualization: `runtime_graph.png`

Compilation and execution commands will be added after implementation.

## 6. Experimental Verification
Experiments will measure algorithm execution time for increasing input sizes and compare measured growth against the theoretical O(n log n) complexity.

## 7. Report
The final LaTeX report will include the formal mathematical abstraction, algorithm, proof of correctness, complexity analysis, experimental results, source citations, and required appendices.
</WritingBlock>

## Step 4 — Start coding `greedy.cpp`

First, let's implement the part that **represents green-light windows**. We'll add sorting and greedy selection after this.

Open `Greedy/greedy.cpp` and enter:

<CodeBlock language="cpp" editable>
#include <iostream>
#include <vector>
using namespace std;

// Represents one candidate green-light window
struct GreenWindow {
    int id;
    int start;
    int finish;
};

int main() {
    vector<GreenWindow> windows = {
        {1, 0, 10},  // Window A
        {2, 2, 5},   // Window B
        {3, 5, 8},   // Window C
        {4, 7, 12},  // Window D
        {5, 8, 14}   // Window E
    };

    cout << "Available Green-Light Windows:\n";

    for (const auto& window : windows) {
        cout << "Window " << window.id
             << ": " << window.start
             << " - " << window.finish
             << " seconds\n";
    }

    return 0;
}
</CodeBlock>

### Understand what you just wrote

- `struct GreenWindow` creates a custom data type holding the ID, start time, and finish time for each window.
- `vector<GreenWindow>` stores multiple windows in a dynamic array.
- `{2, 2, 5}` represents Window B, which begins at 2 seconds and ends at 5 seconds.
- The `for` loop prints every window.
- `const auto&` lets you read each window without copying it.

**Notice:** We haven't implemented the actual greedy selection yet. First, we're making sure we can store and display our input correctly.

### Step 5 — Compile and run

From your project root in the VS Code PowerShell terminal:

```powershell
g++ -std=c++17 Greedy/greedy.cpp -o greedy.exe
.\greedy.exe
```

You should see:

```text
Available Green-Light Windows:
Window 1: 0 - 10 seconds
Window 2: 2 - 5 seconds
Window 3: 5 - 8 seconds
Window 4: 7 - 12 seconds
Window 5: 8 - 14 seconds
```

========== MESSAGE 035 — ASSISTANT — 2026-10-14T15:59:37.822428+00:00 ==========
That means VS Code doesn't recognize `vector` or `GreenWindow`. Most likely, you're missing the required definitions at the top of your file.

### Step 1 — Add this ABOVE `int main()`

```cpp
#include <iostream>
#include <vector>
using namespace std;

struct GreenWindow {
    int id;
    int start;
    int finish;
};
```

Here's why:

- **`#include <vector>`** tells C++ what a vector is.
- **`using namespace std;`** lets you write `vector` instead of `std::vector`.
- **`struct GreenWindow`** defines your custom data type. C++ doesn't know what `GreenWindow` means until you define it.

### Step 2 — Your file should be structured like this

```cpp
#include <iostream>
#include <vector>
using namespace std;

struct GreenWindow {
    int id;
    int start;
    int finish;
};

int main() {
    vector<GreenWindow> windows = {
        {1, 0, 10},
        {2, 2, 5},
        {3, 5, 8},
        {4, 7, 12},
        {5, 8, 14}
    };

    cout << "Available Green-Light Windows:\n";

    for (const auto& window : windows) {
        cout << "Window " << window.id
             << ": " << window.start
             << " - " << window.finish
             << " seconds\n";
    }

    return 0;
}
```

## Step 1 — Understand what we're adding

Right now, your program only displays the five windows:

```text
Window 1: 0 - 10
Window 2: 2 - 5
Window 3: 5 - 8
Window 4: 7 - 12
Window 5: 8 - 14
```

But our greedy algorithm needs to **sort the windows by finishing time** before choosing which ones to schedule.

The correct sorted order is:

| Window ID | Start | Finish |
|---|---:|---:|
| 2 | 2 | 5 |
| 3 | 5 | 8 |
| 1 | 0 | 10 |
| 4 | 7 | 12 |
| 5 | 8 | 14 |

Notice that Window 2 comes first even though Window 1 starts earlier.

## Step 2 — Add the sorting algorithm

Open `Greedy/greedy.cpp`.

First, add this header at the top alongside your existing includes:

```cpp
#include <algorithm>
```

This gives us access to C++'s built-in `sort()` function.

Now, inside `main()`, **right after your vector of windows and before your printing loop**, add:

<CodeBlock language="cpp" editable>
sort(windows.begin(), windows.end(),
    [](const GreenWindow& a, const GreenWindow& b) {
        return a.finish < b.finish;
    });
</CodeBlock>

### What does this code mean?

Let's break it down.

- `sort()` organizes elements in a particular order.
- `windows.begin()` identifies the beginning of the vector.
- `windows.end()` identifies the position just after the last element.
- `[](const GreenWindow& a, const GreenWindow& b)` is a lambda function that compares two windows.
- `return a.finish < b.finish;` tells C++ to place the window with the smaller finish time first.

In other words, you're telling C++:

**"Put the green-light window that finishes earliest before the ones that finish later."**

## Step 4 — What comes next?

After sorting, we'll implement the greedy selection rule:

\[
\boxed{\text{next.start} \geq \text{previous.finish}}
\]

This means we only select another green-light window when it does not overlap the last selected window.

For our example, we expect:

We're **doing that right now!** What we just added is the **first step of the greedy algorithm: sorting the windows.**

There are two main steps:

### Step 1 — Sort the windows (what we're doing now)

We organize the windows from **earliest finish time to latest finish time**.

| Window | Start | Finish |
|---|---:|---:|
| 2 | 2 | 5 |
| 3 | 5 | 8 |
| 1 | 0 | 10 |
| 4 | 7 | 12 |
| 5 | 8 | 14 |

At this point, **we haven't selected any windows yet.** We've only arranged them in the order our greedy algorithm will examine them.

### Step 2 — Select compatible windows (what we'll code next)

Now we go through the sorted windows and decide which to keep.

| Window | Decision | Reason |
|---|---|---|
| 2 | <icon name="check" color="success" inline/> Select | Finishes earliest |
| 3 | <icon name="check" color="success" inline/> Select | Starts when Window 2 finishes |
| 1 | <icon name="x" color="danger" inline/> Skip | Overlaps selected windows |
| 4 | <icon name="x" color="danger" inline/> Skip | Overlaps Window 3 |
| 5 | <icon name="check" color="success" inline/> Select | Starts when Window 3 finishes |

Our final answer will be **Windows 2, 3, and 5**.

Think of it this way:

**Sorting tells us which windows to consider first. The greedy selection loop determines which windows actually get scheduled.**

So right now, we're working on sorting. Once your output displays the windows in the correct order, we'll add the selection loop.

Alright! Let's move to **Step 2: Selecting the correct green-light windows using our greedy algorithm.**

You've already learned how to sort by earliest finishing time. Now we'll use that order to decide which windows to keep.

## 1. Add the selection logic

Open `Greedy/greedy.cpp`.

Make sure your sorting code is already there. Then, **directly after `sort()`**, add:

<CodeBlock language="cpp" editable>
vector<GreenWindow> selected;
int lastFinish = -1;

for (const auto& window : windows) {
    if (window.start >= lastFinish) {
        selected.push_back(window);
        lastFinish = window.finish;
    }
}
</CodeBlock>

## 2. Understand each line

**`vector<GreenWindow> selected;`**

Creates an empty vector that will store only the windows we approve.

**`int lastFinish = -1;`**

Tracks when our most recently selected window ended. We initialize it to `-1` because our example starts at 0 seconds, so the first candidate can be selected.

**`for (const auto& window : windows)`**

Loops through every window in the sorted order.

**`if (window.start >= lastFinish)`**

Checks whether the candidate starts at or after the previous selected window finishes.

**`selected.push_back(window);`**

Adds a compatible window to our approved schedule.

**`lastFinish = window.finish;`**

Updates the finish time so we can check the next candidate.

## 3. Print your selected windows

Under that selection loop, add:

```cpp
cout << "\nSelected Green-Light Windows:\n";

for (const auto& window : selected) {
    cout << "Window " << window.id
         << ": " << window.start
         << " - " << window.finish
         << " seconds\n";
}

cout << "Total selected: " << selected.size() << '\n';
```

In your VS Code terminal:

```powershell
.\greedy.exe

```


**Would `7 >= 8` be true or false?**

That's the exact decision your greedy algorithm makes each time.

========== MESSAGE 055 — ASSISTANT — 2026-10-15T14:04:30.137765+00:00 ==========
Your code is **correct for the five test windows**, and your greedy algorithm is selecting the optimal three windows: **2, 3, and 5**.

Before pushing to GitHub, I recommend making **4 small improvements**. Two are formatting changes, and two make your implementation more reliable for the assignment.

## 1. What needs correcting?

| Code | Issue | Correction |
|---|---|---|
| `lastFinish = -1` | Assumes start times are nonnegative | Use `numeric_limits<int>::min()` |
| `"Window" << window.id` | Prints `Window2` without a space | Change to `"Window " << window.id` |
| Selected windows printed first | Output is harder to follow | Print available windows first |
| Everything inside `main()` | Difficult to reuse for experiments | Put the algorithm in its own function |

Your sorting and compatibility logic are already correct. **Don't change the fundamental greedy strategy.**

## 2. Here is your corrected `greedy.cpp`

I've kept your approach and variable names so you can recognize your own code.

<CodeBlock language="cpp" editable>
#include <iostream>
#include <vector>
#include <algorithm>
#include <limits>
using namespace std;

// Structure to represent a green-light window
struct GreenWindow {
    int id;
    int start;
    int finish;
};

// Greedy algorithm: select the maximum number
// of non-overlapping green-light windows
vector<GreenWindow> greedySelection(vector<GreenWindow> windows) {

    // Step 1: Sort by earliest finishing time
    sort(windows.begin(), windows.end(),
        [](const GreenWindow& a, const GreenWindow& b) {
            return a.finish < b.finish;
        });

    // Step 2: Select compatible windows
    vector<GreenWindow> selected;
    int lastFinish = numeric_limits<int>::min();

    for (const auto& window : windows) {
        if (window.start >= lastFinish) {
            selected.push_back(window);
            lastFinish = window.finish;
        }
    }

    return selected;
}

int main() {
    vector<GreenWindow> windows = {
        {1, 0, 10},
        {2, 2, 5},
        {3, 5, 8},
        {4, 7, 12},
        {5, 8, 14}
    };

    cout << "Available Green-Light Windows:\n";

    for (const auto& window : windows) {
        cout << "Window " << window.id
             << ": " << window.start
             << " - " << window.finish
             << " seconds\n";
    }

    // Run the greedy algorithm
    vector<GreenWindow> selected = greedySelection(windows);

    cout << "\nSelected Green-Light Windows:\n";

    for (const auto& window : selected) {
        cout << "Window " << window.id
             << ": " << window.start
             << " - " << window.finish
             << " seconds\n";
    }

    cout << "\nTotal selected: " << selected.size() << '\n';

    return 0;
}
</CodeBlock>

## 3. Why I changed your code

The most important change is this function:

```cpp
vector<GreenWindow> greedySelection(vector<GreenWindow> windows)
```

Previously, your greedy algorithm was inside `main()`.

Now we've separated it into a reusable function.

This matters because your assignment requires you to experimentally verify the running time.

Later, `experiment.cpp` can call the same algorithm with:

- 1,000 windows
- 10,000 windows
- 50,000 windows
- 100,000 windows

Instead of rewriting the algorithm for every experiment, we can reuse the same function. We'll eventually move it into a shared header/source file so both programs use the same implementation.

### Your sorting is correct

```cpp
sort(windows.begin(), windows.end(),
    [](const GreenWindow& a, const GreenWindow& b) {
        return a.finish < b.finish;
    });
```

This places the windows in ascending finishing-time order.

### Your greedy condition is also correct

```cpp
if (window.start >= lastFinish)
```

The algorithm selects a window only when it starts at or after the previous selected window finishes.

That is exactly what we want.

You're right that **your code works**, but I still recommend fixing the red underlines before we move on to your experiments.

Since your code compiles with `g++` and runs correctly, the problem is most likely **VS Code's IntelliSense configuration**, not your C++ code.

### Why this happens

VS Code uses two separate systems:

- **G++ compiler:** Actually compiles and runs your C++ program. <icon name="check-circle" color="success" inline/>
- **IntelliSense:** Analyzes your code in the editor, provides autocomplete, and displays errors. <icon name="alert-circle" color="danger" inline/>

Your compiler can find `<iostream>`, `<vector>`, and `<algorithm>`, but IntelliSense might not know where those C++ libraries are located.

### Let's fix it

1. Open `greedy.cpp` in VS Code.
2. Press `Ctrl + Shift + P`.
3. Search for **C/C++: Select IntelliSense Configuration**.
4. Select your compiler:
   `C:\msys64\ucrt64\bin\g++.exe`
5. If the red underlines remain, run **C/C++: Reset IntelliSense Database** from the Command Palette.

Also verify that the Microsoft **C/C++** extension is installed and enabled.

\[

## Step 1 — Understand what we're measuring

Our greedy algorithm has two main operations:

| Operation | Time complexity |
|---|---|
| Sort windows by finishing time | \(O(n\log n)\) |
| Select compatible windows | \(O(n)\) |
| **Total** | **\(O(n\log n)\)** |

We want to see how the execution time increases as we give the algorithm more green-light windows.

For example, we'll test:

| Experiment | Number of windows |
|---|---:|
| 1 | 1,000 |
| 2 | 5,000 |
| 3 | 10,000 |
| 4 | 25,000 |
| 5 | 50,000 |
| 6 | 100,000 |
| 7 | 200,000 |

## Step 2 — Open `Greedy/experiment.cpp`

We'll start by creating the same structure used in your original program.

Enter the following code:

<CodeBlock language="cpp" editable>
#include <iostream>
#include <vector>
#include <algorithm>
#include <chrono>
#include <random>
using namespace std;

// Structure to represent a green-light window
struct GreenWindow {
    int id;
    int start;
    int finish;
};

int main() {

    // Number of windows for each experiment
    vector<int> sizes = {
        1000, 5000, 10000, 25000,
        50000, 100000, 200000
    };

    // Random number generator
    mt19937 rng(42);

    // Random starting times
    uniform_int_distribution<int> startDist(0, 1000000);

    for (int n : sizes) {

        vector<GreenWindow> windows;

        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
            int start = startDist(rng);
            int finish = start + 1 + (rng() % 100);

            windows.push_back({i + 1, start, finish});
        }

        cout << "Generated " << windows.size()
             << " green-light windows\n";
    }

    return 0;
}
</CodeBlock>

### Let's understand what's new

**1. `#include <chrono>`**

This library will let us measure execution time later.

**2. `#include <random>`**

This allows us to generate random green-light windows for each experiment.

**3. `mt19937 rng(42);`**

This creates a pseudorandom number generator.

The number `42` is a fixed seed. Using the same seed makes our experiments reproducible.

**4. `uniform_int_distribution<int> startDist(0, 1000000);`**

This generates starting times between 0 and 1,000,000.

**5. `finish = start + 1 + (rng() % 100);`**

This guarantees that every window finishes after it starts, with a duration between 1 and 100 time units.

One improvement we'll make later is using a proper uniform distribution for durations rather than `% 100`.

========== MESSAGE 064 — USER — 2026-10-16T16:14:48.585000+00:00 ==========
what if the timeings off it willl throw everythingoff


========== MESSAGE 065 — ASSISTANT — 2026-10-16T16:14:52.021001+00:00 ==========
Exactly! **If our timing measurements are inaccurate, they could make our results misleading.** You're thinking about an important part of experimental analysis.

But the good news is that we can control most of those issues.

### There are two different types of timing in our project

**1. Green-light timing (the problem we're solving)**

These are the starting and finishing times of traffic signal windows.

For example:

- Window A: 2–5 seconds
- Window B: 5–8 seconds
- Window C: 8–14 seconds

Our algorithm uses these times to determine which windows are compatible.

If these times are incorrect, we could select an invalid schedule. That's why our test data must satisfy conditions such as `finish > start`.

**2. Execution timing (what we're measuring now)**

This measures how long your C++ algorithm takes to run on your computer.

For example, we might measure how many milliseconds it takes to process 10,000 or 100,000 windows.

These measurements can fluctuate because of background applications, CPU activity, and other system processes.

### How we'll make our experiments reliable

We'll use four precautions:

1. **Generate the windows before starting the timer.** Otherwise, we're measuring input generation too.
2. **Use `std::chrono::steady_clock`.** This clock is designed for measuring elapsed time.
3. **Repeat each experiment multiple times.** We can take the median of 10 runs to reduce the influence of unusual measurements.
4. **Compare the growth pattern**, not just individual times, against \(n\log n\).

For example, if one run takes 2 milliseconds and another takes 2.3 milliseconds, that doesn't mean our theoretical analysis is wrong.

### One important distinction

Our experiment is verifying the algorithm's **computational running time**, not whether the traffic signals would work perfectly in the real world.

Also, the fixed random seed (`42`) means we can reproduce the same generated windows when testing.

**So yes, timing matters a lot. But we don't need perfectly identical measurements. We need repeatable experiments that support our theoretical analysis.**

And you're correct about the question I asked earlier: if we include the time spent generating thousands of windows, we wouldn't be measuring just the greedy algorithm anymore.

Next, we'll add the timer around the sorting and selection operations in `experiment.cpp`.

We will add the sorting, selection, and timing **after the window-generation loop**, but still inside `for (int n : sizes)`.

## Step 2 — Add a timer

Immediately after generating the windows, add:

<CodeBlock language="cpp" editable>
auto startTime = chrono::steady_clock::now();

// Sort windows by earliest finishing time
sort(windows.begin(), windows.end(),
    [](const GreenWindow& a, const GreenWindow& b) {
        return a.finish < b.finish;
    });

// Select compatible windows
vector<GreenWindow> selected;
int lastFinish = -1;

for (const auto& window : windows) {
    if (window.start >= lastFinish) {
        selected.push_back(window);
        lastFinish = window.finish;
    }
}

auto endTime = chrono::steady_clock::now();

chrono::duration<double, milli> elapsed = endTime - startTime;

cout << "Input size: " << n
     << " | Selected: " << selected.size()
     << " | Time: " << elapsed.count()
     << " ms\n";
</CodeBlock>

### Understand what we just added

| Code | Purpose |
|---|---|
| `steady_clock::now()` | Captures the starting or ending time |
| `sort()` | Sorts windows by their finishing times |
| `selected.push_back()` | Saves compatible windows |
| `endTime - startTime` | Calculates elapsed execution time |
| `duration<double, milli>` | Converts elapsed time into milliseconds |
| `elapsed.count()` | Gives the measured numerical value |

Notice that the timer starts **after the random windows are generated**. This is intentional, because we only want to measure the sorting and greedy selection.

You're close! Your greedy selection logic is correct, but there are **two important mistakes** in your experiment that we need to fix before you run it.

1. **You accidentally duplicated the window-generation loop.** You have `for (int n : sizes)` twice, one inside the other.
2. **You started the timer after sorting.** That means you're only measuring the selection loop, which is \(O(n)\), instead of the complete greedy algorithm, which is \(O(n\log n)\).

I'll keep your code and structure the same, only correcting what needs to be fixed.

## 1. Your corrected `experiment.cpp`

<CodeBlock language="cpp" editable>
#include <iostream>
#include <vector>
#include <algorithm>
#include <chrono>
#include <random>
using namespace std;

// Represents a green-light window
struct GreenWindow {
    int id;
    int start;
    int finish;
};

int main() {

    // Number of windows for each experiment
    vector<int> sizes = {1000, 5000, 10000, 25000, 50000, 100000, 200000};

    // Random number generator
    mt19937 rng(42);

    // Random start times
    uniform_int_distribution<int> startDist(0, 1000000);

    for (int n : sizes) {
        vector<GreenWindow> windows;

        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
            int start = startDist(rng);
            int finish = start + 1 + (rng() % 100);

            windows.push_back({i + 1, start, finish});
        }

        cout << "Generated " << windows.size()
             << " green-light windows\n";

        // START TIMER BEFORE SORTING
        auto startTime = chrono::steady_clock::now();

        // Sort windows by finish time
        sort(windows.begin(), windows.end(), [](const GreenWindow& a, const GreenWindow& b) {
            return a.finish < b.finish;
        });

        vector<GreenWindow> selected;
        int lastFinish = -1;

        for (const auto& window : windows) {
            if (window.start >= lastFinish) {
                selected.push_back(window);
                lastFinish = window.finish;
            }
        }

        // STOP TIMER AFTER SELECTION
        auto endTime = chrono::steady_clock::now();

        chrono::duration<double, milli> duration = endTime - startTime;

        cout << "Selected " << selected.size()
             << " green-light windows in "
             << duration.count() << " ms\n";
    }

    return 0;
}
</CodeBlock>

You had this inside your original loop:

```cpp
for (int n : sizes) {
    vector<GreenWindow> windows;
    // Generate windows again
}
```

That would cause each input size to trigger another complete set of experiments, generating far more data than necessary.

You only need **one outer loop** over `sizes`.

### Correction 2 — Moved the timer

<box border radius="lg" padding={3} gap={2}>
  <text weight="medium">Correct order for measuring runtime</text>

      **Select compatible windows**

      <text color="secondary" size="xs">O(n)</text>

      <text color="success" size="xs">Measurement ends</text>
    </list-item>
  </list>

You originally put `startTime` immediately before the selection loop. That would miss the sorting operation, which dominates the total running time.

## 2. What we need to improve before submitting

## 3. Let's do the next step: repeat each experiment

First, we're only going to add repetitions. We'll handle the CSV afterward.

Inside `experiment.cpp`, find your timing section that begins with:

```cpp
auto startTime = chrono::steady_clock::now();
```

The goal is to repeat the **sort and selection** for the same input size 10 times.

However, there's an important detail: sorting changes the order of `windows`. If we repeatedly sort the same vector, later runs will be sorting an already sorted vector, which would make the comparison unfair.

So each repetition needs its own copy of the original unsorted windows.

Here's the approach:

<CodeBlock language="cpp" editable>
vector<double> times;

for (int repeat = 0; repeat < 10; repeat++) {

    // Fresh copy of the original unsorted input
    vector<GreenWindow> testWindows = windows;

    auto startTime = chrono::steady_clock::now();

    sort(testWindows.begin(), testWindows.end(),
        [](const GreenWindow& a, const GreenWindow& b) {
            return a.finish < b.finish;
        });

    vector<GreenWindow> selected;
    int lastFinish = -1;

    for (const auto& window : testWindows) {
        if (window.start >= lastFinish) {
            selected.push_back(window);
            lastFinish = window.finish;
        }
    }

    auto endTime = chrono::steady_clock::now();

    chrono::duration<double, milli> duration = endTime - startTime;

    times.push_back(duration.count());
}

// Sort measurements to calculate median
sort(times.begin(), times.end());

double medianTime = (times[4] + times[5]) / 2.0;

cout << "Input size: " << n
     << " | Median runtime: " << medianTime
     << " ms\n";
</CodeBlock>

**Replace your previous timing-and-selection section with this block.** Keep your outer input-generation loop unchanged.

### Understand why we're doing this

The first `sort()` in this block sorts your green-light windows for the algorithm.

The second `sort()` sorts the *measured runtimes*, not the windows. This lets us calculate the median.

For 10 measurements, the median is the average of the 5th and 6th values after sorting:

\[
\text{Median}=\frac{t_5+t_6}{2}
\]

In C++, array indexing starts at zero, so those values are `times[4]` and `times[5]`.

### One note about the benchmark

The code copies the input outside the timer, so the reported time includes sorting and selecting, but not making a copy of the original data. That's the intended measurement.

## 2. Why this matters

- **Outer loop:** Tests seven input sizes (1,000 through 200,000).
- **Inner loop:** Runs the greedy algorithm 10 times for each input size.

We want the program to finish both loops before executing `return 0;`.

\[

========== MESSAGE 077 — ASSISTANT — 2026-10-16T16:27:51.800153+00:00 ==========
Here's your complete, corrected `Greedy/experiment.cpp`. I kept your original code and logic, fixed the brackets, and cleaned up the formatting.

<CodeBlock language="cpp" editable>
#include <iostream>
#include <vector>
#include <algorithm>
#include <chrono>
#include <random>
using namespace std;

// Represents a green-light window
struct GreenWindow {
    int id;
    int start;
    int finish;
};

int main() {

    // Number of windows for each experiment
    vector<int> sizes = {1000, 5000, 10000, 25000, 50000, 100000, 200000};

    // Random number generator
    mt19937 rng(42);

    // Random start times
    uniform_int_distribution<int> startDist(0, 1000000);

    for (int n : sizes) {
        vector<GreenWindow> windows;

        // Generate random green-light windows
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
            int start = startDist(rng);
            int finish = start + 1 + (rng() % 100);

            windows.push_back({i + 1, start, finish});
        }

        cout << "Generated " << windows.size()
             << " green-light windows\n";

        vector<double> times;

        // Repeat each experiment 10 times
        for (int repeat = 0; repeat < 10; repeat++) {

            // Fresh copy of the original unsorted input
            vector<GreenWindow> testWindows = windows;

            // Start timer
            auto startTime = chrono::steady_clock::now();

            // Sort windows by earliest finishing time
            sort(testWindows.begin(), testWindows.end(),
                [](const GreenWindow& a, const GreenWindow& b) {
                    return a.finish < b.finish;
                });

            // Greedy selection
            vector<GreenWindow> selected;
            int lastFinish = -1;

            for (const auto& window : testWindows) {
                if (window.start >= lastFinish) {
                    selected.push_back(window);
                    lastFinish = window.finish;
                }
            }

            // Stop timer
            auto endTime = chrono::steady_clock::now();

            // Calculate elapsed time in milliseconds
            chrono::duration<double, milli> duration = endTime - startTime;

            times.push_back(duration.count());
        }

        // Sort measurements to calculate median
        sort(times.begin(), times.end());

        // Median of 10 measurements
        double medianTime = (times[4] + times[5]) / 2.0;

        cout << "Input size: " << n
             << " | Median runtime: " << medianTime
             << " ms\n\n";

    } // End of outer loop

    return 0;
}
</CodeBlock>

## What this program does

1. Generates seven datasets ranging from 1,000 to 200,000 green-light windows.
2. Runs your greedy sorting and selection algorithm **10 times for each dataset**.
3. Measures execution time in milliseconds.
4. Calculates the median runtime for each dataset.
5. Prints seven median runtime results.

That's **70 timed executions** in total.

Make sure you're on your `greedy` branch, then run:



```text
Generated 5000 green-light windows
Input size: 5000 | Median runtime: ... ms

...

Generated 200000 green-light windows
Input size: 200000 | Median runtime: ... ms
```

The program measures the complete sorting and selection process, which has theoretical time complexity:

\[
\boxed{O(n\log n)}
\]

## Step 3 — Create your experimental graph

We'll use Python and Matplotlib to make a PNG graph suitable for your LaTeX report. This doesn't change your C++ implementation.

<CodeBlock language="python" editable>
import csv
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt

sizes = []
times = []
theoretical = []

with open("Greedy/results.csv", newline="") as file:
    reader = csv.DictReader(file)

    for row in reader:
        sizes.append(int(row["InputSize"]))
        times.append(float(row["MedianTimeMs"]))
        theoretical.append(float(row["NLogN"]))

# Scale the theoretical curve to match
# the measured runtime at the largest input size
scale = times[-1] / theoretical[-1]
scaled_theory = [value * scale for value in theoretical]

plt.figure(figsize=(9, 5))

plt.plot(
    sizes,
    times,
    marker="o",
    label="Measured median runtime"
)

plt.plot(
    sizes,
    scaled_theory,
    linestyle="--",
    label="Scaled O(n log n)"
)

plt.xlabel("Number of green-light windows (n)")
plt.ylabel("Execution time (milliseconds)")
plt.title("Greedy Algorithm: Experimental Runtime Analysis")
plt.legend()
plt.grid(True, alpha=0.3)
plt.tight_layout()

plt.savefig("Greedy/runtime_graph.png", dpi=300)
plt.show()
</CodeBlock>


## Step 4 — Understand your graph

Your graph will contain two curves.

**Measured runtime:** The actual median execution time from your computer, using 10 repetitions for each input size.

**Theoretical curve:** The function

\[
T(n)=c\,n\log_2(n)
\]

where \(c\) is a scaling constant.

We scale the theoretical curve because \(n\log n\) describes growth, not time in milliseconds.

Importantly, matching the curves at the largest input size doesn't prove the complexity. What matters is whether the other measurements show a similar growth pattern. If they don't, report that difference rather than adjusting the data.

========== MESSAGE 081 — ASSISTANT — 2026-10-19T13:32:44.979841+00:00 ==========
Here's the **complete updated `Greedy/experiment.cpp`**. You can replace everything in your current `experiment.cpp` with this code.

I kept your original greedy algorithm, added the CSV functionality, and included the theoretical \(O(n\log n)\) calculation.

<CodeBlock language="cpp" editable>
#include <iostream>
#include <vector>
#include <algorithm>
#include <chrono>
#include <random>
#include <fstream>
#include <cmath>
using namespace std;

// Represents a green-light window
struct GreenWindow {
    int id;
    int start;
    int finish;
};

int main() {

    // Number of windows for each experiment
    vector<int> sizes = {1000, 5000, 10000, 25000, 50000, 100000, 200000};

    // Random number generator
    mt19937 rng(42);

    // Random start times
    uniform_int_distribution<int> startDist(0, 1000000);

    // Create CSV file to save experimental results
    ofstream csv("Greedy/results.csv");

    if (!csv.is_open()) {
        cerr << "Error: Could not open results.csv\n";
        return 1;
    }

    // CSV column headings
    csv << "InputSize,MedianTimeMs,NLogN\n";

    for (int n : sizes) {

        vector<GreenWindow> windows;

        // Generate random green-light windows
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
            int start = startDist(rng);
            int finish = start + 1 + (rng() % 100);

            windows.push_back({i + 1, start, finish});
        }

        cout << "Generated " << windows.size()
             << " green-light windows\n";

        vector<double> times;

        // Repeat each experiment 10 times
        for (int repeat = 0; repeat < 10; repeat++) {

            // Fresh copy of the original unsorted input
            vector<GreenWindow> testWindows = windows;

            // Start timer
            auto startTime = chrono::steady_clock::now();

            // Sort windows by earliest finishing time
            sort(testWindows.begin(), testWindows.end(),
                [](const GreenWindow& a, const GreenWindow& b) {
                    return a.finish < b.finish;
                });

            // Greedy selection
            vector<GreenWindow> selected;
            int lastFinish = -1;

            for (const auto& window : testWindows) {
                if (window.start >= lastFinish) {
                    selected.push_back(window);
                    lastFinish = window.finish;
                }
            }

            // Stop timer
            auto endTime = chrono::steady_clock::now();

            // Calculate elapsed time in milliseconds
            chrono::duration<double, milli> duration = endTime - startTime;

            times.push_back(duration.count());
        }

        // Sort the measurements to calculate the median
        sort(times.begin(), times.end());

        // Median of 10 measurements
        double medianTime = (times[4] + times[5]) / 2.0;

        // Calculate theoretical O(n log n) growth
        double theoretical = n * log2(n);

        // Save results to CSV
        csv << n << ","
            << medianTime << ","
            << theoretical << "\n";

        // Display results
        cout << "Input size: " << n
             << " | Median runtime: " << medianTime
             << " ms\n\n";
    }

    // Close CSV file
    csv.close();

    cout << "Experimental results saved to Greedy/results.csv\n";

    return 0;
}
</CodeBlock>

## Step 3 — Correct your `plot_results.py`

Here is your full corrected code. I kept your original structure and added a check for missing data.

<CodeBlock language="python" editable>
import csv
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt

sizes = []
times = []
theoretical = []

with open("Greedy/results.csv", newline="") as file:
    reader = csv.DictReader(file)

    for row in reader:
        sizes.append(int(row["InputSize"]))
        times.append(float(row["MedianTimeMs"]))
        theoretical.append(float(row["NLogN"]))

# Check that CSV contains experimental results
if not times:
    raise ValueError("results.csv contains no experimental data. Run experiment.exe first.")

# Scale the theoretical curve to the measured
# runtime at the largest input size
scale = times[-1] / theoretical[-1]
scaled_theory = [value * scale for value in theoretical]

plt.figure(figsize=(9, 5))

plt.plot(
    sizes,
    times,
    marker="o",
    label="Measured median runtime"
)

plt.plot(
    sizes,
    scaled_theory,
    linestyle="--",
    label="Scaled O(n log n)"
)

plt.xlabel("Number of green-light windows (n)")
plt.ylabel("Execution time (ms)")
plt.title("Greedy Algorithm: Experimental Runtime Analysis")
plt.legend()
plt.grid(True, alpha=0.3)
plt.tight_layout()

plt.savefig("Greedy/runtime_graph.png", dpi=300)
plt.show()
</CodeBlock>

========== MESSAGE 094 — ASSISTANT — 2026-10-19T14:01:03.379173+00:00 ==========
Perfect! Since your greedy algorithm is working and you've fixed the experimental graph, we're ready for the next requirement: **the proof of correctness (10 points).**

This is one of the most important parts of your COT5405 assignment. Your professor wants you to mathematically prove that your greedy algorithm produces an optimal solution, not just demonstrate that it works on your test cases.

We'll work through the proof first, then put it into your LaTeX report.

## 1. What are we trying to prove?

Your algorithm selects the maximum number of non-overlapping green-light windows.

Remember, your greedy strategy is:

**Always select the compatible green-light window that finishes earliest.**

We need to prove this strategy always produces the maximum number of compatible windows, assuming each candidate window has a valid start and finish time and our objective is to maximize the number of selected windows.

For example:

| Window | Start | Finish |
|---|---:|---:|
| A | 0 | 10 |
| B | 2 | 5 |
| C | 5 | 8 |
| D | 7 | 12 |
| E | 8 | 14 |

Your algorithm selects B, C, and E, giving three windows.

But showing one example isn't a proof. We need to establish that the method works for **every valid input**.

## 2. The exchange argument

We're going to use a technique called an **exchange argument**.

Here's the idea:

Suppose someone already found an optimal schedule, but their first selected window isn't the one our greedy algorithm chooses.

We want to show that we can replace their first window with our earliest-finishing window **without making the schedule worse**.

Let:

- \(G\) = the first window chosen by our greedy algorithm.
- \(O\) = an optimal schedule containing the maximum possible number of compatible windows.
- \(O_1\) = the first window in the optimal schedule.

Because our greedy algorithm selects the earliest-finishing window:

\[
f(G)\leq f(O_1)
\]

This means our selected window ends at the same time as or earlier than the first window in the optimal schedule.

Therefore, we can replace \(O_1\) with \(G\) without introducing any conflicts with the remaining windows.

**Why?** Every later window that starts after \(O_1\) finishes will also start after \(G\) finishes.

The number of selected windows stays the same, so the modified schedule is still optimal.

We can repeat this argument for each remaining window.

That's why our greedy algorithm is optimal.

## 3. Formal proof for your report

Here's a draft of the proof. Review it and make sure you understand each step before including it in your report.

<box border radius="lg" padding={3} gap={2}>
  **Theorem: Optimality of Greedy Green-Light Window Scheduling**

  Let \(W=\{w_1,w_2,\ldots,w_n\}\) be a set of valid candidate green-light windows, where each window \(w_i\) has a start time \(s_i\) and finish time \(f_i\), with \(s_i< f_i\).

  The earliest-finish-time greedy algorithm produces a maximum-cardinality subset of mutually compatible windows.

  **Proof:**

  Let \(g\) be the window with the earliest finishing time, and let \(O=(o_1,o_2,\ldots,o_k)\) be an optimal compatible schedule ordered chronologically.

  By the definition of \(g\),

  \[
  f(g)\leq f(o_1).
  \]

  Construct a new schedule by replacing \(o_1\) with \(g\).

  Since \(g\) finishes no later than \(o_1\), and all subsequent windows start at or after \(f(o_1)\), they also start at or after \(f(g)\). Thus, the new schedule is compatible and contains exactly \(k\) windows.

  Therefore, there exists an optimal schedule containing the greedy choice \(g\).

  After selecting \(g\), the algorithm considers only windows starting at or after \(f(g)\). This remaining problem has the same structure as the original problem.

  Applying the same exchange argument repeatedly shows that every greedy selection can be included in an optimal schedule.

  Therefore, the greedy algorithm selects the maximum possible number of mutually compatible green-light windows. \(\square\)
</box>

## 4. Understand the proof with an example

Suppose an optimal solution begins with Window A, while our greedy algorithm begins with Window B.

<box border radius="lg" padding={3} gap={2}>
  <svg viewBox="0 0 320 109" width="100%">
    {#each [0,2,4,6,8,10,12,14] as t}
      <line x1={35+t*18.5} y1="13" x2={35+t*18.5} y2="91" stroke="currentColor" strokeOpacity=".12"/>
      <text x={35+t*18.5} y="104" fontSize="9" textAnchor="middle" fill="currentColor">{t}</text>
    {/each}
    <text x="9" y="35" fontSize="11" fill="currentColor">A</text>
    <rect x="35" y="19" width="185" height="24" rx="4" fill="#94a3b8" fillOpacity=".65"/>
    <text x="127" y="35" textAnchor="middle" fontSize="11" fill="#1e293b">0–10</text>
    <text x="9" y="71" fontSize="11" fill="currentColor">B</text>
    <rect x="72" y="55" width="55.5" height="24" rx="4" fill="#16a34a" fillOpacity=".85"/>
    <text x="100" y="71" textAnchor="middle" fontSize="11" fill="white">2–5</text>
  </svg>
</box>

Choosing B leaves more available time after it finishes. Replacing A with B cannot prevent later windows from being scheduled, provided those windows were compatible with A.

This is the key idea behind the proof.

## 5. Add the proof to your LaTeX report

For now, create a file named:

`report/greedy_proof.tex`

Paste the following LaTeX into it:

<CodeBlock language="latex" editable>
\subsection{Proof of Correctness}

\textbf{Theorem.}
The earliest-finish-time greedy algorithm produces
a maximum-cardinality set of mutually compatible
green-light windows.

\textbf{Proof.}
Let $g$ be the candidate window with the earliest
finishing time, and let
$O = (o_1, o_2, \ldots, o_k)$
be an optimal schedule ordered chronologically.

Since $g$ has the earliest finishing time,
\[
f(g) \leq f(o_1).
\]

Replace $o_1$ with $g$ in the optimal schedule.
Every subsequent window starts at or after
$f(o_1)$, and therefore also starts at or after
$f(g)$.

The replacement preserves compatibility and the
number of selected windows. Thus, there exists
an optimal schedule containing the greedy choice.

After selecting $g$, the remaining problem
consists of windows starting at or after $f(g)$.
The same exchange argument applies recursively
to this smaller problem.

By induction on the number of remaining windows,
the greedy algorithm produces an optimal schedule.

\hfill $\square$
</CodeBlock>

If you're using Overleaf, your main report can include this section with:

```latex
\input{greedy_proof}
```

This assumes the file is in the same directory as your `main.tex`. If `main.tex` is in a different folder, you'll need to adjust the relative path.

========== MESSAGE 098 — ASSISTANT — 2026-10-19T14:48:06.168372+00:00 ==========
In your `report/greedy_proof.tex` file, **paste the following LaTeX code**. This is your formal proof of correctness, worth 10 points in the assignment.

You don't need to write C++ in this file. LaTeX is used to format the mathematical explanation for your professor.

### `report/greedy_proof.tex`

<CodeBlock language="latex" editable>
\subsection{Proof of Correctness}

\textbf{Theorem.}
The greedy algorithm selects the maximum possible
number of mutually compatible green-light windows
by always choosing the available window with the
earliest finishing time.

\textbf{Proof.}
Let $W = \{w_1, w_2, \ldots, w_n\}$ represent
the set of candidate green-light windows.

Each window $w_i$ has a starting time $s_i$
and finishing time $f_i$, where $s_i < f_i$.

Two consecutive windows are compatible if
the next window starts at or after the
previous window finishes.

Let $g$ be the window with the earliest finishing
time, and let $O = (o_1, o_2, \ldots, o_k)$
be an optimal schedule containing the maximum
number of compatible windows.

Since the greedy algorithm selects the window
with the earliest finishing time,

\[
f(g) \leq f(o_1).
\]

We can replace $o_1$ with $g$ in the optimal
schedule without reducing the number of
selected windows.

This replacement is valid because every
subsequent window in $O$ starts at or after
$f(o_1)$, which is greater than or equal
to $f(g)$.

Therefore, the modified schedule remains
compatible and contains the same number
of windows as the original optimal schedule.

After selecting $g$, the algorithm considers
only windows beginning at or after $f(g)$.

The remaining problem has the same structure
as the original scheduling problem.

By applying the same exchange argument
repeatedly, every greedy choice can be part
of an optimal schedule.

Therefore, the earliest-finish-time greedy
algorithm always produces a maximum-cardinality
set of mutually compatible green-light windows.

\hfill $\square$
</CodeBlock>

## What this proof actually says

The most important argument is:

\[
f(g)\leq f(o_1)
\]

This means the window selected by your greedy algorithm finishes **no later than the first window in an optimal solution**.

Because it finishes no later, replacing that first window cannot interfere with any of the later windows in the optimal schedule.

You can repeat that reasoning until the entire schedule is constructed.

That's why your algorithm is optimal for maximizing the number of compatible windows.


This assumes `main.tex` and `greedy_proof.tex` are in the same folder.

========== MESSAGE 099 — USER — 2026-10-19T14:49:30.296000+00:00 ==========
Theorem: Optimality of Greedy Green-Light Window Scheduling
Let \(W=\{w_1,w_2,\ldots,w_n\}\) be a set of valid candidate green-light windows, where each window \(w_i\) has a start time \(s_i\) and finish time \(f_i\), with \(s_i< f_i\).
The earliest-finish-time greedy algorithm produces a maximum-cardinality subset of mutually compatible windows.
Proof:
Let \(g\) be the window with the earliest finishing time, and let \(O=(o_1,o_2,\ldots,o_k)\) be an optimal compatible schedule ordered chronologically.
By the definition of \(g\),
\[ f(g)\leq f(o_1). \]
Construct a new schedule by replacing \(o_1\) with \(g\).
Since \(g\) finishes no later than \(o_1\), and all subsequent windows start at or after \(f(o_1)\), they also start at or after \(f(g)\). Thus, the new schedule is compatible and contains exactly \(k\) windows.
Therefore, there exists an optimal schedule containing the greedy choice \(g\).
After selecting \(g\), the algorithm considers only windows starting at or after \(f(g)\). This remaining problem has the same structure as the original problem.

========== MESSAGE 100 — ASSISTANT — 2026-10-19T14:49:34.326266+00:00 ==========
**Yes! That's exactly the proof that goes into your `report/greedy_proof.tex` file.** Your mathematical reasoning is correct.

The only thing you need to change is the **LaTeX formatting**. Instead of writing `Theorem:` and `Proof:` as plain text, we can format them properly for your conference-style report.

### Paste this into `report/greedy_proof.tex`

<CodeBlock language="latex" editable>
\subsection{Proof of Correctness}

\noindent\textbf{Theorem: Optimality of Greedy Green-Light Window Scheduling.}

Let $W = \{w_1, w_2, \ldots, w_n\}$ be a set of
valid candidate green-light windows, where each
window $w_i$ has a start time $s_i$ and finish
time $f_i$, with $s_i < f_i$.

The earliest-finish-time greedy algorithm produces
a maximum-cardinality subset of mutually
compatible windows.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Proof.}

Let $g$ be the window with the earliest finishing
time, and let $O = (o_1, o_2, \ldots, o_k)$ be an
optimal compatible schedule ordered chronologically.

By the definition of $g$,

\[
f(g) \leq f(o_1).
\]

Construct a new schedule by replacing $o_1$ with $g$.

Since $g$ finishes no later than $o_1$, and all
subsequent windows start at or after $f(o_1)$,
they also start at or after $f(g)$.

Thus, the new schedule is compatible and contains
exactly $k$ windows.

Therefore, there exists an optimal schedule
containing the greedy choice $g$.

After selecting $g$, the algorithm considers
only windows starting at or after $f(g)$.

This remaining problem has the same structure
as the original problem.

Applying the same exchange argument repeatedly,
or equivalently using induction on the remaining
number of windows, shows that every greedy
selection can be included in an optimal schedule.

Therefore, the greedy algorithm selects the maximum
possible number of mutually compatible green-light
windows.

\hfill $\square$
</CodeBlock>

### Important things to know

- **Don't add** `\documentclass` or `\begin{document}` because this file will become part of your main LaTeX report.
- Your proof uses an **exchange argument**, which is an appropriate technique for proving greedy algorithm optimality.
- This proves optimality for maximizing the **number of compatible candidate windows**, not for minimizing actual traffic congestion.
- Keep this proof and the associated AI prompts in your required AI-use appendix.

Open **`report/greedy_proof.tex`** in VS Code, delete anything currently in that file, and paste the following code exactly.

This is your **proof of correctness (10 points)** for the greedy traffic signal scheduling algorithm.

<CodeBlock language="latex" editable>
\subsection{Proof of Correctness}

\noindent\textbf{Theorem: Optimality of Greedy Green-Light Window Scheduling.}

Let $W = \{w_1, w_2, \ldots, w_n\}$ be a set of valid
candidate green-light windows, where each window
$w_i$ has a start time $s_i$ and finish time $f_i$,
with $s_i < f_i$.

The earliest-finish-time greedy algorithm produces
a maximum-cardinality subset of mutually compatible
green-light windows.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Proof.}

Let $g$ be the window with the earliest finishing
time, and let $O = (o_1, o_2, \ldots, o_k)$ be an
optimal compatible schedule ordered chronologically.

By the definition of $g$,

\[
f(g) \leq f(o_1).
\]

Construct a new schedule by replacing $o_1$ with $g$.

Since $g$ finishes no later than $o_1$, and all
subsequent windows start at or after $f(o_1)$,
they also start at or after $f(g)$.

Thus, the new schedule remains compatible and
contains exactly $k$ windows.

Therefore, there exists an optimal schedule
containing the greedy choice $g$.

After selecting $g$, the algorithm considers only
windows starting at or after $f(g)$.

This remaining problem has the same structure
as the original problem.

By applying the same exchange argument repeatedly
to the remaining windows, the greedy algorithm
can make each selection without reducing the
maximum number of compatible windows.

Therefore, the greedy algorithm selects the
maximum possible number of mutually compatible
green-light windows.

\hfill $\square$
</CodeBlock>

Your `main.tex` is **100% correct!** Don't change anything in that file.

### `greedy_proof.tex`

<CodeBlock language="latex" editable>
\subsection{Proof of Correctness}

\noindent\textbf{Theorem: Optimality of Greedy Green-Light Window Scheduling}

Let $W = \{w_1, w_2, \ldots, w_n\}$ represent a set
of candidate green-light windows. Each window
$w_i$ has a starting time $s_i$ and finishing time
$f_i$, where $s_i < f_i$.

The earliest-finish-time greedy algorithm produces
a maximum-cardinality subset of mutually compatible
green-light windows.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Proof.}

Let $g$ be the window with the earliest finishing
time, and let $O = (o_1, o_2, \ldots, o_k)$ be an
optimal schedule containing the maximum possible
number of compatible windows.

Since the greedy algorithm selects the window
with the earliest finishing time,

\[
f(g) \leq f(o_1).
\]

We can replace $o_1$ with $g$ in the optimal
schedule without reducing the number of
selected windows.

This replacement is valid because every
subsequent window in $O$ starts at or after
$f(o_1)$, which is greater than or equal to $f(g)$.

Therefore, the modified schedule remains
compatible and contains the same number
of windows as the original optimal schedule.

After selecting $g$, the algorithm considers
only windows beginning at or after $f(g)$.

The remaining problem has the same structure
as the original scheduling problem.

By repeatedly applying the same exchange argument
to the remaining windows, every greedy choice
can be included in an optimal schedule.

Therefore, the earliest-finish-time greedy
algorithm always produces a maximum-cardinality
set of mutually compatible green-light windows.

\hfill $\square$
</CodeBlock>

========== MESSAGE 110 — ASSISTANT — 2026-10-19T14:56:01.892185+00:00 ==========
**Yes! Your `greedy_proof.tex` is correct.** ✅

Your proof uses the appropriate mathematical technique, called an **exchange argument**, to demonstrate why the greedy algorithm selects the maximum number of compatible green-light windows.

### What you did correctly

1. **Theorem:** Clearly states what your algorithm guarantees.
2. **Mathematical abstraction:** Defines the set of windows and their starting and finishing times.
3. **Greedy choice:** Explains why selecting the earliest-finishing window is beneficial.
4. **Exchange argument:** Shows that replacing the first window in an optimal schedule with the greedy choice preserves optimality.
5. **Repeated application:** Explains why the same reasoning works for the remaining windows.
6. **Conclusion:** Establishes that the algorithm produces an optimal solution.

### Two small things to check

**1. Remove the triple backticks (` ``` `)** if you actually pasted them into your `.tex` file. Those are Markdown formatting, not LaTeX.

**2. Make the final induction step slightly more explicit.** Your reasoning is sound, but for a graduate algorithms assignment, I recommend replacing:

```latex
By repeatedly applying the same exchange argument
to the remaining windows, every greedy choice
can be included in an optimal schedule.
```

With:

```latex
By induction on the number of remaining windows,
the same exchange argument shows that each greedy
choice preserves the existence of an optimal
solution. The base case of zero remaining windows
is trivially optimal.
```

This makes your correctness argument more rigorous.
