## Part D — The proof you just wrote

This is what you were working on most recently.

You created:

`report/greedy_proof.tex`

Your proof establishes that **the earliest-finish-time greedy algorithm selects the maximum possible number of mutually compatible windows**.

The technique you used is called an *exchange argument*.

Here's the central idea:

Suppose an optimal schedule begins with a different window from the one your greedy algorithm selects.

Because your greedy window finishes no later than the optimal schedule's first window,

\[
f(g)\leq f(o_1)
\]

you can replace that first window with your greedy choice without interfering with the remaining scheduled windows.

This replacement maintains the number of windows selected. Applying the same reasoning to the remaining windows establishes optimality.

**What you learned:** Testing shows that an algorithm works on specific examples. A correctness proof establishes that it works for every valid input under the stated assumptions.

You also created `report/main.tex`, which uses:

```latex
\input{greedy_proof}
```

This tells LaTeX to insert your proof into the compiled report.

## 1. Create `report/greedy_runtime.tex`

This section explains why your algorithm runs in \(O(n\log n)\) time and connects the theory to your actual experimental measurements.

Copy this entire code into `greedy_runtime.tex`:

<CodeBlock language="latex" editable>
\subsection{Running-Time Analysis}

The greedy traffic signal scheduling algorithm
consists of two main operations: sorting the
candidate green-light windows and selecting
a maximum set of compatible windows.

\subsubsection{Theoretical Analysis}

Let $n$ represent the number of candidate
green-light windows.

The algorithm first sorts all windows by their
finishing times using the C++ standard library
sorting function.

The sorting operation requires
$O(n\log n)$ time.

After sorting, the algorithm examines each window
exactly once. If a window starts at or after the
finishing time of the previously selected window,
it is added to the selected set.

Each compatibility check requires $O(1)$ time,
and the selection loop requires $O(n)$ time.

The total running time is therefore:

\[
T(n) = O(n\log n) + O(n)
\]

Since sorting dominates the running time,

\[
\boxed{T(n) = O(n\log n)}
\]

\subsubsection{Experimental Verification}

To verify the theoretical running-time analysis,
the algorithm was implemented in C++ and tested
using randomly generated green-light windows.

Seven input sizes were tested, ranging from
1,000 to 200,000 candidate windows.

For each input size, the algorithm was executed
10 times, and the median execution time was
recorded in milliseconds.

The experiments used the C++ chrono library
with the steady clock to measure execution time.

Random input generation was excluded from
the timed portion of each experiment.

The program was compiled using the
\texttt{-O2} optimization flag.

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering
\caption{Experimental Running-Time Results}
\label{tab:greedy-runtime}
\begin{tabular}{|r|r|}
\hline
Input Size ($n$) & Median Time (ms) \\
\hline
1,000 & 0.03560 \\
5,000 & 0.19195 \\
10,000 & 0.43350 \\
25,000 & 1.10480 \\
50,000 & 2.36325 \\
100,000 & 4.75960 \\
200,000 & 10.90790 \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\subsubsection{Experimental Graph}

Figure~\ref{fig:greedy-runtime} compares
the measured execution times against a
scaled theoretical $O(n\log n)$ curve.

\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{runtime_graph.png}
\caption{Measured median execution time
compared with scaled $O(n\log n)$ growth.}
\label{fig:greedy-runtime}
\end{figure}

The measured execution times increase as
the number of candidate windows increases.

The experimental results follow a growth
pattern consistent with the theoretical
$O(n\log n)$ running-time analysis.

Although individual measurements can vary
due to system activity and hardware effects,
the overall results support the expected
asymptotic complexity.
</CodeBlock>

**Important:** Since this code includes a graph, add the following to your `main.tex`, before `\begin{document}`:

```latex
\usepackage{graphicx}
```

You'll also need to copy `Greedy/runtime_graph.png` into your `report/` folder for the image path above to work.

## 2. `report/greedy_proof.tex`

Your proof was already written correctly. The only necessary formatting fix is to remove the Markdown triple backticks.

<CodeBlock language="latex" editable>
\subsection{Proof of Correctness}

\noindent\textbf{Theorem: Optimality of Greedy Green-Light Window Scheduling}

Let $W = \{w_1, w_2, \ldots, w_n\}$ represent a set
of candidate green-light windows. Each window
$w_i$ has a starting time $s_i$ and finishing time
$f_i$, where $s_i < f_i$.

The earliest-finish-time greedy algorithm produces
a maximum-cardinality subset of mutually compatible
green-light windows.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Proof.}

Let $g$ be the window with the earliest finishing
time, and let $O = (o_1, o_2, \ldots, o_k)$ be an
optimal schedule containing the maximum possible
number of compatible windows.

Since the greedy algorithm selects the window
with the earliest finishing time,

\[
f(g) \leq f(o_1).
\]

We can replace $o_1$ with $g$ in the optimal
schedule without reducing the number of
selected windows.

This replacement is valid because every
subsequent window in $O$ starts at or after
$f(o_1)$, which is greater than or equal to $f(g)$.

Therefore, the modified schedule remains
compatible and contains the same number
of windows as the original optimal schedule.

After selecting $g$, the algorithm considers
only windows beginning at or after $f(g)$.

The remaining problem has the same structure
as the original scheduling problem.

By repeatedly applying the same exchange argument
to the remaining windows, every greedy choice
can be included in an optimal schedule.

Therefore, the earliest-finish-time greedy
algorithm always produces a maximum-cardinality
set of mutually compatible green-light windows.

\hfill $\square$
</CodeBlock>

## 3. `report/greedy_runtime.tex`

This is your existing running-time analysis, preserving your measured data.

<CodeBlock language="latex" editable>
\subsection{Running-Time Analysis}

The greedy traffic signal scheduling algorithm
consists of two main operations: sorting the
candidate green-light windows and selecting
a maximum set of compatible windows.

\subsubsection{Theoretical Analysis}

Let $n$ represent the number of candidate
green-light windows.

The algorithm first sorts all windows by their
finishing times using the C++ standard library
sorting function.

The sorting operation requires
$O(n\log n)$ time.

After sorting, the algorithm examines each window
exactly once. If a window starts at or after the
finishing time of the previously selected window,
it is added to the selected set.

Each compatibility check requires $O(1)$ time,
and the selection loop requires $O(n)$ time.

The total running time is therefore:

\[
T(n) = O(n\log n) + O(n)
\]

Since sorting dominates the running time,

\[
\boxed{T(n) = O(n\log n)}
\]

\subsubsection{Experimental Verification}

To verify the theoretical running-time analysis,
the algorithm was implemented in C++ and tested
using randomly generated green-light windows.

Seven input sizes were tested, ranging from
1,000 to 200,000 candidate windows.

For each input size, the algorithm was executed
10 times, and the median execution time was
recorded in milliseconds.

The experiments used the C++ chrono library
with the steady clock to measure execution time.

Random input generation was excluded from
the timed portion of each experiment.

The program was compiled using the
\texttt{-O2} optimization flag.

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering
\caption{Experimental Running-Time Results}
\label{tab:greedy-runtime}
\begin{tabular}{|r|r|}
\hline
Input Size ($n$) & Median Time (ms) \\
\hline
1,000 & 0.03560 \\
5,000 & 0.19195 \\
10,000 & 0.43350 \\
25,000 & 1.10480 \\
50,000 & 2.36325 \\
100,000 & 4.75960 \\
200,000 & 10.90790 \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\subsubsection{Experimental Graph}

Figure~\ref{fig:greedy-runtime} compares
the measured execution times against a
scaled theoretical $O(n\log n)$ curve.

\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{runtime_graph.png}
\caption{Measured median execution time
compared with scaled $O(n\log n)$ growth.}
\label{fig:greedy-runtime}
\end{figure}

The measured execution times increase as
the number of candidate windows increases.

The experimental results follow a growth
pattern consistent with the theoretical
$O(n\log n)$ running-time analysis.

Although individual measurements can vary
due to system activity and hardware effects,
the overall results support the expected
asymptotic complexity.
</CodeBlock>

## Updated `report/ai_usage_appendix.tex`

<CodeBlock language="latex" editable>
\appendix

\section{AI Tool Usage and Disclosure}

\subsection{AI Tool Used}

OpenAI ChatGPT was used to assist with the
development, testing, documentation, and
mathematical analysis of the greedy
traffic signal scheduling algorithm.

The AI tool provided explanations, example
implementations, debugging assistance,
experimental benchmarking code, and LaTeX
formatting guidance.

\subsection{Problem Selection and Abstraction}

\noindent\textbf{Original User Prompt:}

\begin{quote}
what if i change it to the max amount of time
you can have lights running at the same time
to avoid cloging traffic
\end{quote}

\noindent\textbf{AI-Generated Result:}

ChatGPT explained the concept of coordinated
traffic signals, also known as a green wave.

It explained that minimizing real-world
congestion involves several factors,
including vehicle travel times, signal
offsets, and competing traffic movements.

ChatGPT suggested a simplified interval
scheduling problem that could be solved
optimally using a greedy algorithm.

Each candidate green-light window is
represented by a start and finish time.

The objective is to select the maximum
number of mutually compatible windows.

The suggested greedy strategy is to sort
the windows by finishing time and select
each compatible window in that order.

The tool also explained that maximizing
the number of compatible windows does not
necessarily minimize real-world congestion.

\subsection{Greedy Algorithm Implementation}

\noindent\textbf{Original User Prompt:}

\begin{quote}
okay lets se
\end{quote}

\noindent\textbf{AI-Generated Result:}

ChatGPT provided the following selection
logic and explained each operation.

\begin{verbatim}
vector<GreenWindow> selected;
int lastFinish = -1;

for (const auto& window : windows) {
    if (window.start >= lastFinish) {
        selected.push_back(window);
        lastFinish = window.finish;
    }
}
\end{verbatim}

The generated explanation stated that
the selected vector stores compatible
windows.

The lastFinish variable records the
ending time of the previously selected
window.

The compatibility condition ensures
that a new window is selected only when
it starts at or after the previous
selected window finishes.

\subsection{Code Review and Debugging}

\noindent\textbf{Original User Prompt:}

\begin{quote}
before i push it chekc my code correct what
needs to be correctedso i know im pushing
the full correct file
\end{quote}

\noindent\textbf{AI-Generated Result:}

ChatGPT reviewed the submitted C++ code.

It identified output formatting,
initialization, and code organization
improvements.

The suggestions included adding a space
between the word Window and its ID,
printing available windows before
selected windows, and improving
initialization for more general inputs.

The user subsequently requested that
the original algorithm structure be
preserved.

The final implementation retained
the original sorting and selection
strategy.

\subsection{Experimental Runtime Analysis}

\noindent\textbf{Original User Prompt:}

\begin{quote}
give me the whole code
\end{quote}

\noindent\textbf{AI-Generated Result:}

ChatGPT generated a complete version of
the C++ runtime experiment program.

The program generated random green-light
windows and measured the time required
for sorting and greedy selection.

Seven input sizes were used:

\begin{verbatim}
vector<int> sizes = {
    1000, 5000, 10000, 25000,
    50000, 100000, 200000
};
\end{verbatim}

ChatGPT generated the following timing
and repetition logic:

\begin{verbatim}
vector<double> times;

for (int repeat = 0; repeat < 10; repeat++) {

    vector<GreenWindow> testWindows = windows;

    auto startTime = chrono::steady_clock::now();

    sort(testWindows.begin(), testWindows.end(),
        [](const GreenWindow& a, const GreenWindow& b) {
            return a.finish < b.finish;
        });

    vector<GreenWindow> selected;
    int lastFinish = -1;

    for (const auto& window : testWindows) {
        if (window.start >= lastFinish) {
            selected.push_back(window);
            lastFinish = window.finish;
        }
    }

    auto endTime = chrono::steady_clock::now();

    chrono::duration<double, milli> duration =
        endTime - startTime;

    times.push_back(duration.count());
}

sort(times.begin(), times.end());

double medianTime =
    (times[4] + times[5]) / 2.0;
\end{verbatim}

The generated program measured the complete
sorting and selection procedure.

Input generation was excluded from the
timed section.

Each experiment was repeated ten times,
and the median was calculated.

\subsection{CSV Result Generation}

\noindent\textbf{Original User Prompt:}

\begin{quote}
send me the updated experiment code so i can
just paste itbecause imgetting errors
\end{quote}

\noindent\textbf{AI-Generated Result:}

ChatGPT generated an updated experiment
program that included CSV output.

The relevant generated code was:

\begin{verbatim}
ofstream csv("Greedy/results.csv");

if (!csv.is_open()) {
    cerr << "Error: Could not open results.csv\n";
    return 1;
}

csv << "InputSize,MedianTimeMs,NLogN\n";
\end{verbatim}

The generated program calculated the
theoretical growth function:

\begin{verbatim}
double theoretical = n * log2(n);

csv << n << ","
    << medianTime << ","
    << theoretical << "\n";
\end{verbatim}

The resulting CSV contained the input size,
median execution time, and theoretical
n log n value.

\subsection{Experimental Graph Generation}

\noindent\textbf{Original User Prompt:}

\begin{verbatim}

\end{verbatim}

\noindent\textbf{AI-Generated Result:}

ChatGPT explained how to execute the

Additional assistance was provided

and resolving an empty-data error.

The plotting code generated with
AI assistance included:

\begin{verbatim}
scale = times[-1] / theoretical[-1]

scaled_theory = [
    value * scale for value in theoretical
]

plt.figure(figsize=(9, 5))

plt.plot(
    sizes,
    times,
    marker="o",
    label="Measured median runtime"
)

    sizes,

    linestyle="--",
    label="Scaled O(n log n)"

    "Greedy Algorithm: Experimental Runtime Analysis"
)

plt.tight_layout()

plt.show()
\end{verbatim}

The generated graph compares measured
execution times with a scaled theoretical
O(n log n) curve.

\subsection{Correctness Proof}

\noindent\textbf{Original User Prompt:}

\begin{quote}
so show me exactly what i should write
\end{quote}

\noindent\textbf{AI-Generated Result:}

ChatGPT generated the LaTeX correctness
proof using an exchange argument.

The central inequality was:

\[
f(g) \leq f(o_1).
\]

Here, $g$ is the earliest-finishing
window and $o_1$ is the first window
in an optimal compatible schedule.

The proof explained that replacing
$o_1$ with $g$ preserves compatibility
because $g$ finishes no later.

The same argument can be applied
to the remaining compatible windows.

Therefore, the greedy algorithm
produces a maximum-cardinality
compatible schedule.

The generated LaTeX proof is included
in the report as
\texttt{greedy\_proof.tex}.

\subsection{LaTeX Report Assistance}

\noindent\textbf{Original User Prompt:}

\begin{quote}
so write me the greedy_runtime.text
and i thought therw was an ai usage apendix
\end{quote}

\noindent\textbf{AI-Generated Result:}

ChatGPT generated LaTeX sections for
the running-time analysis and
AI usage disclosure.

The theoretical analysis was:

\[
T(n) = O(n\log n) + O(n).
\]

Since the sorting operation dominates
the linear selection loop, the total
complexity is:

\[
T(n) = O(n\log n).
\]

ChatGPT also generated a LaTeX table
of experimental results and code for
including the runtime graph.

The resulting report section is stored
in \texttt{greedy\_runtime.tex}.

\subsection{Verification and Experimental Results}

The greedy scheduling program was compiled
and executed using the MSYS2 G++ compiler.

The experiment was compiled with the
\texttt{-O2} optimization flag.

Seven input sizes were tested, with
ten repetitions per input size.

The median execution times were:

\begin{center}

Input Size & Median Runtime (ms) \\
\hline
1000 & 0.03560 \\
5000 & 0.19195 \\
10000 & 0.43350 \\
25000 & 1.10480 \\
50000 & 2.36325 \\
100000 & 4.75960 \\
200000 & 10.90790 \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

The recorded measurements were consistent
with the expected O(n log n)
running-time growth.

<CodeBlock language="latex" editable>
\subsection{Real-World Problem Description}

Traffic congestion is a common problem in urban
areas, particularly at intersections controlled
by traffic signals. Poorly coordinated traffic
signals can cause vehicles to stop repeatedly,
increase waiting times, and contribute to
congestion along major roads.

One issue occurs when traffic signals release
vehicles from one intersection while another
intersection farther down the road is unable
to accommodate the incoming traffic.

This problem motivated the investigation of
traffic signal scheduling using a greedy
algorithm.

For this project, we consider a simplified
traffic signal scheduling problem.

A traffic management system is assumed to
provide a collection of candidate green-light
time windows for a particular traffic movement.

Each window represents a time interval during
which a green-light opportunity could be
scheduled.

The scheduling system must select a subset
of these windows such that no two selected
windows overlap.

The objective is to maximize the total
number of compatible green-light windows.

This simplified model assumes that all
candidate windows have equal importance,
each window is indivisible, and the safety
and feasibility of every candidate window
have already been verified.

Although real-world traffic signal
coordination involves additional factors,
including traffic volume, vehicle speeds,
pedestrian crossings, and intersection
capacity, this abstraction allows us
to study an optimization problem that
can be solved optimally using a
greedy algorithm.

\subsection{Mathematical Abstraction}

The traffic signal scheduling problem
is modeled as a set of time intervals.

Let

\[
W = \{w_1, w_2, \ldots, w_n\}
\]

represent the set of candidate green-light
windows.

Each window is represented as

\[
w_i = (s_i, f_i)
\]

where:

\begin{itemize}
    \item $s_i$ represents the starting
    time of window $i$.

    \item $f_i$ represents the finishing
    time of window $i$.

    \item $n$ represents the total number
    of candidate windows.
\end{itemize}

Every window must satisfy

\[
s_i < f_i.
\]

Two windows are compatible when they
do not overlap.

For two windows $w_i$ and $w_j$,
where $w_i$ is scheduled before $w_j$,
compatibility requires

\[
f_i \leq s_j.
\]

This means that the next window may
begin only after the previous
selected window has finished.

The goal is to find a subset

\[
S \subseteq W
\]

that maximizes the number of
selected windows:

\[
\max |S|
\]

subject to the constraint that
all selected windows are
mutually compatible.

This is a maximum-cardinality
interval scheduling problem.

The greedy algorithm solves this
problem by sorting candidate
windows according to their
finishing times and repeatedly
selecting the earliest-finishing
window that does not conflict
with previously selected windows.

The result is an optimal subset
containing the maximum possible
number of compatible green-light
windows under the stated assumptions.
</CodeBlock>

## Step 1 — Create `report/greedy_algorithm.tex`

In VS Code, right-click your `report` folder, select **New File**, and name it:

`greedy_algorithm.tex`

Paste this code into the file:

<CodeBlock language="latex" editable>
\subsection{Greedy Algorithm Design}

The proposed algorithm uses the earliest-finish-time
greedy strategy to select the maximum number of
mutually compatible green-light windows.

Each candidate window is represented by a starting
time and a finishing time.

The algorithm first sorts all candidate windows
in ascending order of finishing time.

It then processes each window and selects it
if its starting time is greater than or equal
to the finishing time of the previously
selected window.

Otherwise, the window is skipped.

\subsubsection{Algorithm Pseudocode}

\begin{verbatim}
GREEDY-WINDOW-SCHEDULING(W)

Input:
    W = set of candidate green-light windows

Output:
    S = maximum set of compatible windows

1. Sort W by ascending finishing time
2. S = empty list
3. lastFinish = -infinity

4. For each window w in W:

5.     If w.start >= lastFinish:

6.         Add w to S

7.         lastFinish = w.finish

8. Return S
\end{verbatim}

\subsubsection{Greedy Choice}

The greedy choice is to select the available
compatible window with the earliest finishing time.

This strategy is used because selecting a window
that finishes earlier leaves at least as much
time available for future windows as selecting
one that finishes later.

By repeating this selection process, the
algorithm constructs a schedule containing
the maximum number of compatible windows.

The correctness of this strategy is established
using an exchange argument in the proof of
correctness section.

\subsubsection{Explanation in the Traffic Signal Domain}

In the context of traffic signal scheduling,
the algorithm receives a collection of
candidate green-light time windows.

Each window represents a potential period
during which a particular traffic movement
could receive a green signal.

The scheduling system first arranges these
opportunities according to their ending times.

The earliest-finishing opportunity is
selected first.

The system then considers the remaining
opportunities and approves each one only
if it does not overlap an already selected
green-light window.

For example, consider the following
candidate windows:

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{|c|c|c|}
\hline
Window & Start (s) & Finish (s) \\
\hline
1 & 0 & 10 \\
2 & 2 & 5 \\
3 & 5 & 8 \\
4 & 7 & 12 \\
5 & 8 & 14 \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

After sorting the windows by finishing
time, the algorithm selects windows
2, 3, and 5.

Window 2 finishes at 5 seconds,
window 3 begins at 5 seconds,
and window 5 begins at 8 seconds
when window 3 finishes.

These windows are compatible because
none of their time intervals overlap.

The algorithm therefore selects three
compatible green-light windows.

This approach optimizes the number
of selected candidate windows under
the simplified scheduling model.

It does not directly optimize vehicle
throughput, signal safety, or traffic
congestion across a real road network.
\end{verbatim}
</CodeBlock>

**One correction before saving:** The final `\end{verbatim}` above is unnecessary because the pseudocode environment was already closed. Delete that final line from your `.tex` file.

## Step 3 — Understand what you just documented

Your pseudocode describes the same logic as your actual C++ implementation.

| Pseudocode | C++ implementation |
|---|---|
| Sort windows | `sort(windows.begin(), windows.end(), ...)` |
| Create empty result | `vector<GreenWindow> selected;` |
| Track last finish | `int lastFinish = -1;` |
| Check compatibility | `window.start >= lastFinish` |
| Approve window | `selected.push_back(window)` |
| Update finish time | `lastFinish = window.finish` |

The pseudocode uses negative infinity to describe the initial state generally, whereas your current C++ code uses `-1` because your generated start times are nonnegative.

Let me explain what `\input{greedy_problem}` means.

### 1. What does `\input{greedy_problem}` do?

Think of `main.tex` as the main program in C++.

When you write:

```latex
\input{greedy_problem}
```

You're telling Overleaf:

**"Find the file named `greedy_problem.tex` and insert its contents here in my report."**

You don't need to copy the contents of `greedy_problem.tex` into `main.tex`. Overleaf does that automatically when compiling.

Your screenshot confirms that `greedy_problem.tex` exists, so this command should work.

The biggest issue is probably your **C++ code listings and pseudocode**, which were originally formatted for a single-column report.

### Step 1 — Adjust the code formatting

In Overleaf, open `main.tex`.

Find your existing `\lstset` section and replace it with:

<CodeBlock language="latex" editable>
\lstset{
    basicstyle=\ttfamily\scriptsize,
    breaklines=true,
    breakatwhitespace=false,
    frame=single,
    numbers=left,
    numberstyle=\tiny,
    numbersep=4pt,
    showstringspaces=false,
    columns=fullflexible,
    keepspaces=true
}
</CodeBlock>

This makes your code slightly smaller and allows long lines to wrap within the IEEE columns.

### Step 2 — Fix the experimental graph width

In `greedy_runtime.tex`, make sure your graph uses:

```latex
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{runtime_graph.png}
```

This tells LaTeX to fit the graph inside the available column.

### Step 3 — Recompile

Click **Recompile** and check whether the spacing looks better.

Don't manually add lots of `\vspace` commands to force things together. IEEE controls much of the page layout automatically.

## What changed?

| Setting | Purpose |
|---|---|
| `\documentclass[conference]{IEEEtran}` | Uses IEEE conference formatting |
| `\scriptsize` | Makes code fit better in narrow columns |
| `breaklines=true` | Wraps long code lines |
| `numbersep=4pt` | Reduces space between line numbers and code |
| `columns=fullflexible` | Helps code fit inside columns |
| `keepspaces=true` | Preserves indentation in code |
